below. In what follows, given a function
\varpi:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
, we say that the colored stochastic six-vertex model
\bm{\eta}
on the quadrant
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
has boundary data
\varpi
if the below holds. For each integer
x\leq 0
, an arrow of color
\varpi(x)
horizontally enters the quadrant through
(0,1-x)
and, for each integer
x>0
, an arrow of color
\varpi(x)
vertically enters the quadrant through
(x,0)
. We say that the boundary data for this model matches the initial condition for a colored ASEP
\bm{\xi}=\big(\xi_{t}(x)\big)
if
\varpi=\xi_{0}
.

Proposition B.2
.

There exists a constant
C>1
such that the following holds. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
T\geq 0
be real numbers; let
U\leq V
be integers; let
\mathcal{T}\subset[0,T]
denote a finite set of real numbers; and let
\bm{\xi}=\big(\xi_{t}(x)\big)
denote a colored ASEP with at most
n
colors. Further let
\bm{\eta}=\big(\eta_{y}(x)\big)
denote a colored stochastic six-vertex model on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
with parameters
(b_{1},b_{2})=(\varepsilon L,\varepsilon R)
, whose boundary data matches the initial data of
\bm{\xi}
. It is possible to couple
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\eta}
so that, with probability at least
1-C(V-U+1)(T+1)^{2}|\mathcal{T}|\varepsilon^{1/8}
, we have

(B.1)

\displaystyle\xi_{t}(x)=\eta_{\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}\big(x+\lfloor t\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor\big),\qquad\text{for each $(x,t)\in\llbracket U,V\rrbracket\times\mathcal{T}$ with $x+\lfloor t\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor>0$}.

To prove
B.2
, it will be useful to introduce notation for the randomness defining the colored stochastic six-vertex model (this discussion also appears in a slightly different, though equivalent, form in
[
3
, Section 2.3]
and
[
64
, Section 2.2]
). To that end, for any vertex
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we associate Bernoulli random variables
\chi_{x,y},\psi_{x,y}\in\{0,1\}
with
\mathbb{P}[\chi_{x,y}=1]=b_{1}
and
\mathbb{P}[\psi_{x,y}=1]=b_{2}
. Given all of these random variables, the dynamics of the colored stochastic six-vertex model are defined as follows. Fix a vertex
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
, and suppose that colored six-vertex arrow configurations have been assigned to each
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
with
x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}<x+y
; this fixes the colors
a=a(x,y)
and
b=b(x,y)
of the arrows vertically and horizontally entering
(x,y)
, respectively. Then define the colors
c=c(x,y)
and
d=d(x,y)
of the arrows vertically and horizontally exiting
(x,y)
, respectively, according to the below procedure.

(1)

If
a=b
, then set
c=d=a=b
.

(2)

If
a>b
, then set
(c,d)=(a,b)
if
\chi_{x,y}=1
and
(c,d)=(b,a)
if
\chi_{x,y}=0
.

(3)

If
a<b
, then set
(c,d)=(a,b)
if
\psi_{x,y}=1
and
(c,d)=(b,a)
if
\psi_{x,y}=0
.

This provides a way of sampling the colored stochastic six-vertex model defined above. In what follows, for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, define the increasing integer sequences
\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)=\big(\mathfrak{s}_{1}(x),\mathfrak{s}_{2}(x),\ldots)
and
\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)=\big(t_{1}(x),t_{2}(x),\ldots\big)
, such that
\mathfrak{s}\in\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)
if and only if
\chi_{x+\mathfrak{s}-1,\mathfrak{s}}>0
, and
\mathfrak{t}\in\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)
if and only if
\psi_{x+\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}}>0
(here, the offsets
x+\mathfrak{s}
and
x+\mathfrak{t}
are introduced to match with (
B.1
)). Observe that the
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)\big)
and
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)\big)
(over all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
) together determine all
(\chi_{x,y},\psi_{x,y})_{x,y>0}
.

Before proceeding, we record the following lemma that provides a finite speed of discrepancy bound for the colored stochastic six-vertex model (and is analogous to
B.1
).

Lemma B.3
.

There exists a constant
C>1
such that the following holds. Let
T\geq 0
,
K>1
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be real numbers; set
(b_{1},b_{2})=(\varepsilon L,\varepsilon R)
; and let
U\leq V
be integers. Further let
\bm{\xi}_{y}=\big(\xi_{y}(x)\big)_{x>0}
and
\bm{\eta}_{y}=\big(\eta_{y}(x)\big)_{x>0}
denote two colored stochastic six-vertex models with parameters
(b_{1},b_{2})
and boundary data
\varpi^{\bm{\xi}}:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
and
\varpi^{\bm{\eta}}:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
, respectively. Assume that
\varpi^{\bm{\xi}}(x)=\varpi^{\bm{\eta}}(x)
for each
x\in\llbracket U-CKT,V+CKT\rrbracket
. Then it is possible to couple
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\eta}
such that, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-K(T+1)}
, we have
\xi_{y}(x)=\eta_{y}(x)
for each
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\llbracket 1,T\varepsilon^{-1}\rrbracket
satisfying
U\leq x-y\leq V
.

Proof.

Couple
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\eta}
under the same processes
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)\big)
and
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)\big)
, and define the strip

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\big\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\llbracket 1,T\varepsilon^{-1}\rrbracket:U\leq x-y\leq V\big\}.

Then
\xi_{y_{0}}(x_{0})\neq\eta_{y_{0}}(x_{0})
holds for some
(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathcal{R}
only if there exists some
(x_{1},y_{1})
on the
x
-axis or
y
-axis with
U-CKT\leq x_{1}-y_{1}\leq V+CKT
, such that a colored path entering the quadrant through
(x_{1},y_{1})
could (for some trajectories of the remaining colored paths) enter
\mathcal{R}
under the above randomness
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)\big)
and
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)\big)
. This is contained in the event on which there exists an integer
k\geq 0
; a sequence of integers
w_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
with
|w_{i}-w_{i-1}|=1
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
; and a sequence of positive integers
0\leq\mathfrak{r}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{r}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\mathfrak{r}_{k}\leq T\varepsilon^{-1}
such that
\mathfrak{r}_{i}\in\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(w_{i})\cup\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(w_{i})
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, and one of the following two possibilities holds. The first is that
w_{0}\leq U-CKT\leq U\leq w_{k}
; the second is that
w_{0}\geq V+CKT\geq V\geq w_{k}
.

Now, observe that the entry differences of any
\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)\cup\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)
are distributed as independent geometric random variables with parameter
1-(1-\varepsilon L)(1-\varepsilon R)\leq\varepsilon(L+R)
. Hence, the probabilty of each of the above possibilities is bounded by the probability that of a sum of
\lfloor T\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
geometric random variables with parameter
\varepsilon(R+L)
travels a distance of at least
CKT
. A Chernoff bound implies that the latter is at most
Ce^{-K(T+1)}
if
C
is sufficiently large, establishing the lemma.
∎

B.3.
Proof of
B.2

In this section we establish
B.2
. Its proof will use the below lemma, which is shown in Section
B.4
below. It indicates that the processes
\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x)
and
\bm{\mathsf{T}}(x)
from Section
B.1
can be coupled to nearly coincide (after scaling) with the
\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)
and
\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)
from Section
B.2
, on a long interval, with high probability. In what follows, we recall the notation from those sections, assocating the parameters
(L;R)
with the
(\bm{\mathsf{S}};\bm{\mathsf{T}})
processes and
(\varepsilon L;\varepsilon R)
with the
(\bm{\mathfrak{s}};\bm{\mathfrak{t}})
ones.

Lemma B.4
.

There exists a coupling between
\big(\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x);\bm{\mathsf{T}}(x)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x);\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and a constant
C>1
such that, for any real numbers
U_{0}\leq V_{0}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
T\geq 0
, the following statements all hold with probability at least
1-C(V_{0}-U_{0}+1)(T+1)\varepsilon^{1/4}
.

(1)

For any index pair
(\mathsf{R},\mathfrak{r})\in\big\{(\mathsf{S},\mathfrak{s}),(\mathsf{T},\mathfrak{t})\big\}
and each pair
(i,x)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket U_{0},V_{0}\rrbracket
such that
\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)\leq T
or
\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)\leq T\varepsilon^{-1}
, we have
\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)\leq\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)\leq\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)+\varepsilon^{1/2}<T
.

(2)

For distinct triples
(\mathsf{R},i,x),(\mathsf{R}^{\prime},i^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in\{\mathsf{S},\mathsf{T}\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket U_{0},V_{0}\rrbracket
such that
\mathsf{R}_{i}(x),\mathsf{R}_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})<T
, we have
\big|\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)-\mathsf{R}_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\big|>2\varepsilon^{1/2}
.

Proof of
B.2
.

We will assume for notational convenience that the set of times
\mathcal{T}=\{T\}
(from which the proof of the proposition for general
\mathcal{T}
quickly follows by a union bound). Set
K=\lceil\varepsilon^{-1/8}\rceil
, and let
C_{0}>1
denote the maximum of the constants
C
from
B.1
,
B.3
, and
B.4
.

We first use the finite speed of discrepancy bounds (
B.1
and
B.3
) to “cut off” the initial data for
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\eta}
. To that end, let
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}=\big(\xi_{t}^{\prime}(x)\big)
denote a colored ASEP with initial data
\xi_{0}^{\prime}(x)=\xi_{0}(x)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{x\in[U-C_{0}KT,V+C_{0}KT]}
, and let
\bm{\eta}^{\prime}=\big(\eta_{y}^{\prime}(x)\big)
denote a colored stochastic six-vertex model with parameters
(b_{1},b_{2})=(\varepsilon L,\varepsilon R)
, whose boundary data matches the initial data of
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}
. By
B.1
and
B.3
, we may couple
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}
, and also
\bm{\eta}
and
\bm{\eta}^{\prime}
, so that with probability at least
1-2C_{0}e^{-K}\geq 1-16C_{0}\varepsilon
we have
\xi_{t}(x)=\xi_{t}^{\prime}(x)
for each
(x,t)\in\llbracket U,V\rrbracket\times[0,T]
, and
\eta_{y}(x)=\eta_{y}^{\prime}(x)
for each
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\llbracket 1,T\varepsilon^{-1}\rrbracket
with
U\leq x-y\leq V
. Hence, we may replace
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\eta}
by
\bm{\xi}^{\prime}
and
\bm{\eta}^{\prime}
, respectively, so we will assume in what follows that
\bm{\xi}=\bm{\xi}^{\prime}
and
\bm{\eta}=\bm{\eta}^{\prime}
.

Next define the process
\bm{\zeta}=\big(\zeta_{y}(x)\big)
from
\bm{\eta}
by setting
\zeta_{y}(x)=\eta_{y}(x+y)
(see (
B.1
)) for each
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
. Recall from Section
B.1
and Section
B.2
that
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\zeta}
are determined from the processes
\big(\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x);\bm{\mathsf{T}}(x)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x);\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
, respectively; it will be useful to restrict these processes to a bounded subset of
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. To that end, recall for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}
that
\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x)\cup\bm{\mathsf{T}}(x)
is given by the ringing times of an exponential clock of rate
L+R
. Hence, the trajectory of the rightmost particle of nonzero color in
\bm{\xi}
is stochastically dominated by a random walk starting at
V+C_{0}KT
, that jumps one space to the right whenever an exponential clock of rate
L+R
rings. Therefore, a Chernoff bound implies (after increasing
C_{0}
if necessary) that this particle remains left of
V+2C_{0}KT
with probability at least
1-C_{0}e^{-K}\geq 1-8C_{0}\varepsilon
. Similarly, the leftmost particle of nonzero color in
\bm{\xi}
remains right of
U-2C_{0}KT
with probability at least
1-8C_{0}\varepsilon
.

We may apply analogous reasoning to
\bm{\zeta}
. In particular, for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, the differences of the entries in
\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)\cup\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)
are given by mutually independent geometric random variables of parameter
1-(1-\varepsilon L)(1-\varepsilon R)\leq\varepsilon(L+R)
. Hence, recalling the diagonal shifts in the definitions of
\bm{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{t}}
from Section
B.2
, the trajectory of the rightmost path (of nonzero color) in
\bm{\zeta}
is stochastically dominated by a random walk starting at
V+C_{0}KT
that jumps to the right according to a geometric random variable of parameter
\varepsilon(L+R)
. Therefore, a Chernoff bound implies (after increasing
C_{0}
if necessary) that this path remains to the left of
V+2C_{0}KT
with probability at least
1-C_{0}e^{-K}\geq 1-8C_{0}\varepsilon
. Similarly, the leftmost path of nonzero color in
\bm{\zeta}
remains right of
U-2C_{0}KT
with probability at least
1-8C_{0}\varepsilon
. Together, these facts yield
\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{E}]\geq 1-32C_{0}\varepsilon
, where
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{E}_{1}\cap\mathscr{E}_{2}
and we have denoted the events

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{1}=\big\{\xi_{t}(x)=0,\quad\text{for all $x\notin\llbracket U-2C_{0}KT,V+2C_{0}KT\rrbracket$ and $t\in[0,T]$}\big\};

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{2}=\big\{\zeta_{y}(x)=0,\quad\text{for all $(x,y)\in\llbracket U-2C_{0}KT,V+2C_{0}KT\rrbracket\times\llbracket 0,T\varepsilon^{-1}\rrbracket$ with $x+y>0$}\big\}.

Now,
B.4
gives a coupling between
\big(\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x);\bm{\mathsf{T}}(x)\big)
and
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x);\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)\big)
such that the following holds on an event
\mathscr{F}
with
\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{F}]\geq 1-C_{0}(V-U+4C_{0}KT+1)(T+1)\varepsilon^{1/4}
. First, for any index pair
(\mathsf{R},\mathfrak{r})\in\big\{(\mathsf{S},\mathfrak{s}),(\mathsf{T},\mathfrak{t})\big\}
and each pair
(i,x)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket U-2C_{0}KT,V+2C_{0}KT\rrbracket
with
\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)\leq T
, we have

(B.2)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)\leq\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)\leq\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)+\varepsilon^{1/2}<T.

Second, for any distinct triples
(\mathsf{R},i,x),(\mathsf{R}^{\prime},i^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in\{\mathsf{S},\mathsf{T}\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket U-2C_{0}KT,V+2C_{0}KT\rrbracket
with
\mathsf{R}_{i}(x),\mathsf{R}_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\leq T
, we have

(B.3)

\displaystyle\big|\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)-\mathsf{R}_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\big|>2\varepsilon^{1/2}.

We claim on
\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{F}
that the ASEP and stochastic six-vertex configurations coincide, namely,

(B.4)

\displaystyle\xi_{T}(x)=\zeta_{\lfloor T/\varepsilon\rfloor}(x),\qquad\text{for each $x\in\mathbb{Z}$ with $x+\lfloor T\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor>0$}.

To verify this, first observe on
\mathscr{E}
that
(\bm{\zeta}_{t})_{t\leq T}
and
(\bm{\xi}_{y})_{y\leq T/\varepsilon}
only depend on
\big(\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x),\bm{T}(x)\big)
and
\big(\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x),\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)\big)
for
x\in\llbracket U-2C_{0}KT,V+2C_{0}KT\rrbracket
, respectively. Let
\mathsf{R}_{1}<\mathsf{R}_{2}<\cdots<\mathsf{R}_{m}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{1}<\mathfrak{r}_{2}<\cdots<\mathfrak{r}_{m^{\prime}}
be such that

\displaystyle[0,T]\cap\bigcup_{x=\lceil U-2C_{0}KT\rceil}^{\lfloor V-2C_{0}KT\rfloor}\big(\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x)\cup\bm{T}(x)\big)=\{\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{R}_{m}\};

\displaystyle\llbracket 1,T\varepsilon^{-1}\rrbracket\cap\bigcup_{x=\lceil U-2C_{0}KT\rceil}^{\lfloor V-2C_{0}KT\rfloor}\big(\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)\cup\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)\big)=\{\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{r}_{2},\ldots,\mathfrak{r}_{m^{\prime}}\}.

For each
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, let
w_{i}\in\llbracket U-2C_{0}KT,V+2C_{0}KT\rrbracket
be such that
\mathsf{R}_{i}\in\bm{\mathsf{S}}(w_{i})\cup\bm{\mathsf{T}}(w_{i})
. Then, by (
B.2
) and (
B.3
), we have that
m^{\prime}=m
and for each
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
that
\mathfrak{r}_{i}\in\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(w_{i})\cup\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(w_{i})
and

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{1}-\varepsilon^{1/2}\leq\mathfrak{r}_{1}\leq\mathsf{R}_{1}<\mathsf{R}_{2}-\varepsilon^{1/2}\leq\mathfrak{r}_{2}\leq\mathsf{R}_{2}<\cdots\leq\mathsf{R}_{m}-\varepsilon^{1/2}\leq\mathfrak{r}_{m}\leq\mathsf{R}_{m}<T-\varepsilon^{1/2}.

Now, observe if
y\notin\{\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{r}_{2},\ldots,\mathfrak{r}_{m}\}
then
\eta_{y+1}(x+1)=\eta_{y}(x)
holds for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, as all paths in
\bm{\eta}
proceed one step horizontally and one step vertically; thus,
\bm{\zeta}_{y+1}=\bm{\zeta}_{y}
. Similarly, if
t\notin\{\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{R}_{m}\}
then
\bm{\xi}_{t}=\bm{\xi}_{t^{-}}
. Hence, to show (
B.4
), it suffices to show for each
(i,x)\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}
that
\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{i}}(x)=\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{i}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(x)
.

We do this by induction on
i\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, where we set
\mathsf{R}_{0}=0
. It holds it
i=0
, since the boundary data of
\bm{\eta}
matches that of
\bm{\xi}
. Hence, let us assume it holds for all
i\leq m_{0}-1
for some integer
m_{0}\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and verify it holds for
i=m_{0}
. By (
B.5
) and the inductive hypothesis (with the above discussion that
\bm{\zeta}_{y+1}=\bm{\zeta}_{y}
if
y\notin\{\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{r}_{2},\ldots,\mathfrak{r}_{m}\}
and
\bm{\xi}_{t}=\bm{\xi}_{t^{-}}
if
t\notin\{\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{R}_{m}\}
), we have
\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}^{-}}(x)=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}-1}}(x)=\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}-1}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(x)=\bm{\zeta}_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}/\varepsilon-1\rfloor}(x)
for each
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. Next, if
\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}\in\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(w_{m_{0}})
, then it follows from the discussion in Section
B.2
that
\eta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}}(w_{m_{0}}+\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1)=\eta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1}(w_{m_{0}}+\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1)
, as then the path of color
\eta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1}(w_{m_{0}}+\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1)
entering
(w_{m_{0}}+\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1,\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}})
proceeds one step vertically and no steps horizontally; thus,

(B.6)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}-1}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(w_{m_{0}})=\zeta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1}(w_{m_{0}})=\zeta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}}(w_{m_{0}}-1)=\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(w_{m_{0}}-1),

where in the first and last equality we again used (
B.5
). By (
B.5
) and (
B.3
), we then also have
\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}\in\bm{\mathsf{S}}(w_{m_{0}})
, and so
\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}-1}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(w_{m_{0}})=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}-1}}(w_{m_{0}})=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}^{-}}(w_{m_{0}})=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}}(w_{m_{0}}-1)
. This coincides with (
B.6
); the proof that
\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(x)=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}}(x)
for all other
x\in\mathbb{Z}
is entirely analogous. This verifies the statement at
i=m_{0}
in this case.

If instead
\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}\in\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(w_{m_{0}})
, then
\eta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}}(w_{m_{0}}+\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}+1)=\eta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1}(w_{m_{0}}+\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1)
, as then the path of color
\eta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1}(w_{m_{0}}+\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1)
at
(w_{m_{0}}+\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1,\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1)
proceeds two steps horizontally and one step vertically. So,

(B.7)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}-1}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(w_{m_{0}})=\zeta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}-1}(w_{m_{0}})=\zeta_{\mathfrak{r}_{m_{0}}}(w_{m_{0}})=\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(w_{m_{0}}+1).

By (
B.5
) and (
B.3
), we then also have
\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}\in\bm{\mathsf{T}}(w_{m_{0}})
. Hence,
\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}-1}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(w_{m_{0}})=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}-1}}(w_{m_{0}})=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}^{-}}(w_{m_{0}})=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}}(w_{m_{0}}+1)
. This again coincides with (
B.7
), and the proof that
\zeta_{\lfloor\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}/\varepsilon\rfloor}(x)=\xi_{\mathsf{R}_{m_{0}}}(x)
for all other
x\in\mathbb{Z}
is entirely analogous. This confirims (
B.4
).

Thus, (
B.1
) holds on
\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{F}
. Together with the fact that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{F}]

\displaystyle\geq 1-32C_{0}\varepsilon-C_{0}(V-U+4C_{0}KT+1)(T+1)\varepsilon^{1/4}

\displaystyle\geq 1-40C_{0}^{2}(V-U+1)(T+1)^{2}K\varepsilon^{1/4}\geq 1-80C_{0}^{2}(V-U+1)(T+1)^{2}\varepsilon^{1/8},

this establishes the proposition.
∎

B.4.
Proof of
B.4

In this section we establish
B.4
as a quick consequence of the two lemmas below.

Lemma B.5
.

For any real number
A>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(A)>1
such that, if
\bm{R}=(r_{1},r_{2},\ldots)
denotes the ringing times in increasing order for an exponential clock with parameter
A
, then the following two statements hold.

(1)

For any real numbers
T\geq 0
and
K\geq 1
, we have
\mathbb{P}[r_{\lfloor CK(T+1)\rfloor}\geq T]\geq 1-Ce^{-K(T+1)}
.

(2)

For any real numbers
0<\delta\leq 1\leq B
, we have
\mathbb{P}\big[\min_{1\leq i\leq B}(r_{i+1}-r_{i})\geq\delta\big]\geq 1-AB\delta
.

Proof.

First observe that the event on which
r_{\lfloor C(T+1)\rceil}\geq T
is that on which the sum of
\big\lfloor CK(T+1)\big\rfloor
independent exponential random variables is at least
T
. By a Chernoff bound, this is at least
1-Ce^{-K(T+1)}
if
C=C(A)>1
is sufficiently large, which verifies the first statement of the lemma. Further observe for any integer
i\geq 1
that, since
r_{i+1}-r_{i}
is an exponential random variable of parameter
A
, we have
\mathbb{P}[r_{i+1}-r_{i}\leq\delta]=1-e^{-A\delta}\leq A\delta
. This, together with a union bound over
i\in\llbracket 1,B\rrbracket
, implies the second statement of the lemma.
∎

Lemma B.6
.

Let
A>0
and
\delta\in(0,1)
be real numbers; let
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
denote a geometric random variable with
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{g}=k]=A\delta(1-A\delta)^{k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
; and let
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
denote an exponential random variable with
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{e}>x]=e^{-Ax}
for each
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. It is possible to couple
\mathfrak{e}
and
\mathfrak{g}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\mathfrak{g}=\lfloor\delta^{-1}\mathfrak{e}\rfloor\big]\geq 1-12A\delta.

Proof.

We may assume that
\delta<(2A)^{-1}
, for otherwise
1-12A\delta<0
. It suffices to show that

(B.8)

\displaystyle e^{-A^{2}S\delta^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[S\delta\leq\mathfrak{e}<(S+1)\delta\big]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{g}=S]\leq(1+2A\delta)\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[S\delta\leq\mathfrak{e}<(S+1)\delta\big],

for any integer
S\geq 0
, or equivalently that

(B.9)

\displaystyle e^{-A^{2}S\delta^{2}}\leq\displaystyle\frac{A\delta(1-A\delta)^{S}}{e^{-AS\delta}(1-e^{-A\delta})}\leq 1+2A\delta.

Indeed, given (
B.8
), it would follow that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{S=0}^{\infty}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big[S\delta\leq\mathfrak{e}<(S+1)\delta\big]-\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{g}=S]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 2\displaystyle\sum_{S=0}^{\infty}(1-e^{-A^{2}S\delta^{2}}+A\delta)\cdot e^{-AS\delta}(1-e^{-A\delta})

\displaystyle\leq 2A^{2}\delta^{2}\displaystyle\sum_{S=0}^{\infty}(AS\delta+1)e^{-AS\delta}

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\frac{2A^{2}\delta^{2}}{1-e^{-A\delta}}+\displaystyle\frac{2A^{3}\delta^{3}}{(1-e^{-A\delta})^{2}}\leq 12A\delta,

where in the first equality we used the explicit probability distribution for
\mathfrak{e}
; in the second we used the facts that
1-e^{-A^{2}S\delta^{2}}\leq A^{2}S\delta^{2}
and that
1-e^{-A\delta}\leq A\delta
; in the third we bounded the sums
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}r^{i}=(1-r)^{-1}
and
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}ir^{i}=r(1-r)^{-2}\leq(1-r)^{-2}
at
r=e^{-A\delta}\in(0,1)
; and in the fourth we bounded
1-e^{-A\delta}\geq A\delta/2
(as
\delta<(2A)^{-1}
). This implies that it is possible to couple
\mathfrak{e}
and
\mathfrak{g}
so that
\mathfrak{g}\leq\delta^{-1}\mathfrak{e}<\mathfrak{g}+1
, or equivalently that
\mathfrak{g}=\lfloor\delta^{-1}\mathfrak{e}\rfloor
, with probability at least
1-12A\delta
.

It therefore remains to confirm (
B.9
). To that end, since
1-e^{-A\delta}\leq A\delta
and
1-e^{-A\delta}\geq A\delta(1-A\delta)\geq A\delta(1+2A\delta)^{-1}
(the latter as
\delta<(2A)^{-1}
), observe that

(B.10)

\displaystyle 1\leq\displaystyle\frac{A\delta}{1-e^{-A\delta}}\leq 1+2A\delta.

We also have since
\log(1-x)\leq-x
for each
x\geq 0
, since
\log(1-x)\geq-x(1+x)
for
x\in(0,1/2)
, and since
\delta<(2A)^{-1}
that

(B.11)

\displaystyle e^{-A^{2}S\delta^{2}}\leq e^{AS\delta}(1-A\delta)^{S}\leq 1.

Combining (
B.10
) and (
B.11
) yields (
B.9
) and thus the lemma. ∎

Proof of
B.4
.

Let
C_{0}
denote the constant
C
from
B.5
; we will assume throughout this proof that
\varepsilon<C_{0}^{-4}(T+1)^{-4}
, for otherwise
1-C(V_{0}-U_{0}+1)(T+1)\varepsilon^{1/4}\leq 0
for any
C>C_{0}
. Let us first bound the cardinalities of
\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x)\cap[0,T]
and
\bm{T}(x)\cap[0,T]
, with high probability. To that end, observe by the first part of
B.5
(with
A\in\{L,R\}
and
K=C_{0}^{-1}(T+1)^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/4}\geq 1
) that there exist constants
c>0
and
C_{1}>1
such that
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathsf{S}_{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1/4}\rfloor}(x)\geq T\big]\geq 1-c^{-1}e^{-c\varepsilon^{-1/4}}\geq 1-C_{1}\varepsilon
, and similarly
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathsf{T}_{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1/4}\rfloor}(x)\geq T\big]\geq 1-C_{1}\varepsilon
, both hold for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. Together with a union bound, it follows that
\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{A}_{1}]\geq 1-2C_{1}\varepsilon(V_{0}-U_{0}+1)
, where

(B.12)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{1}=\bigcap_{x=\lceil U_{0}\rceil}^{\lfloor V_{0}\rfloor}\Big\{\#\big\{\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x)\cap[0,T]\big\}\leq\varepsilon^{-1/4}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\#\big\{\bm{\mathsf{T}}(x)\cap[0,T]\big\}\leq\varepsilon^{-1/4}\Big\}.

We may therefore restrict to the event
\mathscr{A}_{1}
in what follows.

On
\mathscr{A}_{1}
, there are at most
\varepsilon^{-1/4}
entries in any
\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x)\cap[0,T]
or
\bm{\mathsf{T}}(x)\cap[0,T]
; their differences are exponential random variables with parameters
L
and
R
, respectively. Moreover, the differences between consecutive entries of any
\bm{\mathfrak{s}}(x)
and
\bm{\mathfrak{t}}(x)
are geometric random variables of parameters
b_{1}=1-\varepsilon L
and
b_{2}=1-\varepsilon R
, respectively. Hence, fixing an integer
x\in\llbracket U,V\rrbracket
and an index
(\mathsf{R},\mathfrak{r})\in\big\{(\mathsf{S},\mathfrak{s}),(\mathsf{T},\mathfrak{t})\big\}
, and applying
B.6
to the at most
\varepsilon^{-1/4}
differences in
\bm{\mathsf{R}}(x)
, it follows that we may couple
\bm{\mathsf{R}}(x)\cap[0,T]=\big(\mathsf{R}_{1}(x),\mathsf{R}_{2}(x),\ldots,\mathsf{\mathsf{R}}_{m}(x)\big)
with
\bm{\mathfrak{r}}(x)\cap\llbracket 1,T\varepsilon^{-1}\rrbracket=\big(\mathfrak{r}_{1}(x),\mathfrak{r}_{2}(x),\ldots,\mathfrak{r}_{m^{\prime}}(x)\big)
so that with probability
1-12(R+L)\varepsilon^{3/4}
we have
m\leq m^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)\leq\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)\leq\varepsilon\big(\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)+\varepsilon^{-1/4}\big)\leq\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)+\varepsilon^{1/2}
, for each
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
. After increasing
C_{0}
if necessary, we further have that
\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{m^{\prime}}(x)<T-\varepsilon^{1/2}
with probability at least
1-C_{0}\varepsilon^{1/2}
(since the probability that
\mathfrak{r}_{m^{\prime}}(x)
lies in any fixed interval at size
\varepsilon^{-1/2}+1
is at most
(R+L)\varepsilon\cdot(\varepsilon^{-1/2}+1)=2(R+L)\varepsilon^{1/2}
), and so
m=m^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)\leq\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)\leq\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{i}(x)+\varepsilon^{1/2}<T
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
. Applying a union bound over
x\in\llbracket U_{0},V_{0}\rrbracket
gives the first statement of the lemma.

To establish the second, observe that the law of
\bigcup_{x=\lceil U_{0}\rceil}^{\lfloor V_{0}\rfloor}\big(\bm{\mathsf{S}}(x)\cup\bm{\mathsf{T}}(x)\big)
coincides with that of the ringing times of an exponential clock of rate
(R+L)\cdot\big(\lfloor V_{0}\rfloor-\lceil U_{0}\rceil+1\big)\leq(V_{0}-U_{0}+1)(R+L)
. Hence the second part of
B.5
implies, for any real number
B\geq 1
, that with probability at least
1-2(V_{0}-U_{0}+1)(R+L)B\varepsilon^{1/2}
we have
\big|\mathsf{R}_{i}(x)-\mathsf{R}_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\big|>2\varepsilon^{1/2}
, for any distinct triples
(\mathsf{R},i,x),(\mathsf{R}^{\prime},i^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in\{\mathsf{S},\mathsf{T}\}\times\llbracket 1,B\rrbracket\times\llbracket U_{0},V_{0}\rrbracket
. Applying this at
B=\varepsilon^{-1/4}
, using our restriction to
\mathscr{A}_{1}
, and applying a union bound then yields the second part of the lemma.
∎
